  55 \title{On the Last Kervaire Invariant Problem}
  56 \author{Weinan Lin, Guozhen Wang, and Zhouli Xu}
  57 \address{Shanghai Center for Mathematical Sciences, Fudan University, Shanghai, China, 200433}
  58 \email{linweinan@fudan.edu.cn}
  59 \address{Shanghai Center for Mathematical Sciences, Fudan University, Shanghai, China, 200433}
  60 \email{wangguozhen@fudan.edu.cn}
  61 \address{UCLA Department of Mathematics, Los Angeles, CA 90095-1555, USA}
  62 \email{xuzhouli@ucla.edu}
  63 \dedicatory{Dedicated to Mark Mahowald}
  64 \date{}
  65 
  66 \begin{document}
  67 \begin{abstract}
  68 We prove that the element $h_6^2$ is a permanent cycle in the Adams spectral sequence. As a result, we establish the existence of smooth framed manifolds with Kervaire invariant one in dimension 126, thereby resolving the final case of the Kervaire invariant problem.
  69 
  70 Combining this result with the theorems of Browder, Mahowald--Tangora, Barratt--Jones--Mahowald, and Hill--Hopkins--Ravenel, we conclude that smooth framed manifolds with Kervaire invariant one exist in and only in dimensions  $2, 6, 14, 30, 62$, and $126$.
  71 % Keywords: \Groebner{} basis, Ext groups, Steenrod algebra
  72 \end{abstract}
  73 
  74 \maketitle
  75 % \tableofcontents
  76 % \blfootnote{The author is supported by China Postdoctoral Science Foundation 2021TQ0015 and the Fundamental Research Funds for the Central Universities, Peking University}
  77 
  78 \section{Introduction}
  79 
  80 %[Recollection to $HF_2$-synthetic spectra.]
  81 
  82 %[Relationship between $En$-pages and $E_\infty(\nu X/\lambda^{n-1})$. We talk about maps between $E_n$-pages and extensions.]
  83 
  84 %[Rigidity, total differential]
  85 
  86 %[Examples and pictures]
  87 
  88 %[Choices of letters]
  89 
  90 %[https://arxiv.org/pdf/2202.11305]
  91 
  92 %\huge
  93 
  94 For a smooth framed manifold in dimension $4k+2$, the Kervaire invariant, taking values in $\mathbb{F}_2$, determines whether the manifold could be converted into a homotopy sphere via surgery -- it takes value 0 if the manifold can be converted to a homotopy sphere and 1 otherwise. Formally speaking, the Kervaire invariant is defined as the Arf invariant of the quadratic refinement of the intersection pairing in the cohomology of the manifold with $\mathbb{F}_2$ coefficients. Using this invariant, Kervaire \cite{Kervaire} discovered a PL-manifold in dimension 10 that does not admit any smooth structure. 
  95 
  96 The Kervaire invariant problem seeks to identify the dimensions in which there exist  framed smooth manifolds with Kervaire invariant one. In these dimensions, exactly half of the cobordism classes of framed manifolds have Kervaire invariant one, and the other half have Kervaire invariant 0. The Kervaire invariant problem is closely related to many problems in differential topology, especially Kervaire--Milnor's classification theorem \cite{KervaireMilnor} on exotic smooth structures on spheres. 
  97 
  98 %Browder \cite{Browder} proves that there exists a framed manifold in dimension $n$ if and only if $n=2^{j+1}-2$ and that the element $h_j^2$ in the Adams spectral sequence survives to the $E_\infty$-page, converging to a nonzero homotopy class in the stable homotopy groups of spheres in dimension $n=2^{j+1}-2$.
  99 
 100 In this paper we prove the following Theorem~\ref{thm:main}.
 101 
 102 \begin{theorem} \label{thm:main}
 103     There exist framed manifolds of Kervaire invariant one in dimension 126.
 104 \end{theorem}
 105 
 106 Together with previous results by Browder \cite{Browder}, Mahowald--Tangora \cite{MahowaldTangora}, Barratt--Jones--Mahowald \cite{BJMtheta5}, and Hill--Hopkins--Ravenel \cite{HHR}, this is the last case of the Kervaire invariant problem.
 107 
 108 \begin{corollary}
 109   The dimensions that there exist framed manifolds of Kervaire invariant one are 2, 6, 14, 30, 62, and 126.  
 110 \end{corollary}
 111 
 112 In dimensions 2, 6, and 14, the product of spheres $S^1 \times S^1, \ S^3 \times S^3, S^7 \times S^7$ can be framed to have Kervarie invariant one.
 113 In dimension 30, an explicit framed manifold of Kervarie invariant one was constructed by J.Jones in \cite{Jones30}. For dimensions 62 and 126, we would like to comment that no explicit manifold of Kervarie invariant one was known, although $50\%$ of all framed manifolds in these two dimensions have Kervarie invariant one.
 114 
 115 Our Theorem \ref{thm:main} is a consequence of Browder's theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:browder}) and the following main theorem of this paper, Theorem~\ref{thm:h_6_sq}. 
 116 
 117 \begin{theorem}[Browder \cite{Browder}] \label{thm:browder}
 118    There exist framed manifolds in dimension $n$ of Kervaire invariant one if and only if
 119    \begin{enumerate}
 120        \item $n=2^{j+1}-2$, and
 121        \item the element $h_j^2$ in the Adams spectral sequence survives to the $E_\infty$-page.
 122    \end{enumerate}
 123 \end{theorem}
 124 
 125 
 126 The Adams spectral sequence \cite{Adams58} converges to the stable homotopy groups of spheres, which, by Pontryagin's theorem, correspond to framed manifolds up to framed cobordism. When the element $h_j^2$ survives (see below for an introduction to the Adams spectral sequence and the element $h_j^2$), the homotopy classes it detects are denoted by $\theta_j \in \pi_{2^{j+1}-2}$. Browder showed that these classes correspond to framed manifolds with Kervaire invariant one. 
 127 
 128 \begin{theorem}[Theorem~\ref{thm:126survives}] \label{thm:h_6_sq}\label{thm:h62}
 129     The element $h_6^2$ survives to the $E_\infty$-page in the Adams spectral sequence.
 130 \end{theorem}
 131 
 132 In addition to Theorem \ref{thm:main}, further implications of Theorem~\ref{thm:h_6_sq}, particularly in manifold topology and unstable homotopy theory, can be found in \cite{MillerHHR, MahowaldRemark, HHR, BJMinduction}, among others. 
 133 
 134 
 135 Recall that the 2-primary Adams spectral sequence has the following form.
 136 $$E_2^{s,t} = \Ext^{s, t}_A(\mathbb{F}_2, \mathbb{F}_2) \Longrightarrow \pi_{t-s} {S^0},$$
 137 $$d_r: E_r^{s,t} \longrightarrow E_r^{s+r, t+r-1}.$$
 138 Here the $E_2$-page is the cohomology of the mod 2 Steenrod algebra A, $S^0$ is the 2-completed sphere spectrum, and the spectral sequence converges to the 2-completed stable homotopy groups of spheres. In general, for a spectrum $X$, its $\HF$-Adams spectral sequence is denoted by $E_r^{*,*}(X)$, converging to the 2-completed homotopy groups of $X$.
 139 
 140 For the sphere, Adams \cite{Adams58} computed the Adams $1$-line: 
 141 $$\Ext^{1,t}_A(\mathbb{F}_2, \mathbb{F}_2) = \begin{cases}
 142  	\mathbb{F}_2, \ \text{if} \ t = 2^j \ \text{for some} \ j \geq 0, \\
 143  	0, \ \text{otherwise}.
 144 \end{cases}
 145 $$
 146 Denote by $h_j$ the generator of $\mathbb{F}_2$ in the bidegree $(s, t)=(1, 2^j)$. Adams \cite{Adams60} proved that the element $h_j$ survives in the Adams spectral sequence if and only if $j \geq 3$, resolving the famous Hopf invariant problem. In fact, Adams proved that
 147 $$d_2(h_j) = h_0h_{j-1}^2 \neq 0, \ \text{for} \ j \geq 4.$$
 148 
 149 The Kervaire invariant element $h_j^2$ lives on the Adams 2-line, which is generated by elements of the form $\{h_ih_j\}$ that are subject to the relations $h_ih_{i+1} = 0$. Notably, $h_6^2$ was the last unknown element on the Adams 2-line whose survival in the Adams spectral sequence remained uncertain prior to our work. As a corollary of Theorem~\ref{thm:h_6_sq}, we have:
 150 
 151 \begin{corollary} \label{cor:2line}
 152     On the Adams 2-line, the only non-trivial elements that survive in the Adams spectral sequence are:
 153     $$h_0h_2, \ h_0h_3, \ h_2h_4, \ h_1h_j \ (j \geq 3), \ h_j^2 \ (j \leq 6).$$
 154 \end{corollary}
 155 
 156 \begin{proof}
 157     From Adams's Hopf invariant one differentials, Lin's computations of the Adams 4-line \cite{LinExt4}, and the Leibniz rule, we know that the only elements that survive to the Adams $E_3$-page are
 158     $$h_0h_2, \ h_0h_3, \ h_2h_4, \ h_2h_5, \ h_3h_5, \ h_3h_6, \ h_1h_j \ (j \geq 3), \ h_j^2 \ (j \leq 6).$$
 159 May \cite{Maythesis} proved that the first three elements and $h_j^2$ for $j \leq 3$ survive. Mahowald--Tangora \cite{MahowaldTangora} proved that $h_2h_5$ supports a nonzero $d_3$-differential, $h_3h_5$ supports a nonzero $d_4$-differential, and $h_4^2$ survives. Isaksen--Wang--Xu \cite{IWX} proved that $h_3h_6$ supports a nonzero $d_3$-differential. Mahowald \cite{Mahowaldetaj} proved that $h_1h_j$ for $j \geq 3$ survive. Barratt--Jones--Mahowald \cite{BJMtheta5} proved that $h_5^2$ survives (see \cite{Xu, IWX} for alternative proofs). Hill--Hopkins--Ravenel \cite{HHR} proved that $h_j^2$ supports nonzero differentials for $j \geq 7$ (the targets of these differentials are still unknown). Finally, by Theorem~\ref{thm:h_6_sq}, $h_6^2$ survives.   
 160 \end{proof}
 161 
 162 Before Hill--Hopkins--Ravenel's proof on the non-existence of $\theta_j$ for $j \geq 7$, Barratt--Jones--Mahowald \cite{BJMinduction} had an inductive argument trying to establish the existence of all $\theta_j$: They proved that if there exists a $\theta_j$ satisfying $2\cdot \theta_j = 0, \ \theta_j^2 = 0$, then there exists a $\theta_{j+1}$ satisfying $2\cdot \theta_{j+1} = 0$. In particular, the problem of whether a $\theta_{j+1}$ of order 2 exists is known as the strong Kervaire invariant problem. It was known that $2\cdot \theta_j = 0$ for $j \leq 5$ (see \cite{MahowaldTangora} for $j=4$ and \cite{Xu, IWX} for $j=5$). Although we prove that $\theta_6$ exists in this paper, the following questions are still open:
 163 
 164 \begin{question} \label{que:2theta6}
 165     Does there exist a $\theta_6$ that has order 2?
 166 \end{question}
 167 
 168 \begin{question} \label{que:theta5sq}
 169     Does there exist a $\theta_5$ such that $\theta_5^2 = 0$?
 170 \end{question}
 171 
 172 By Barratt--Jones--Mahowald's theorem \cite{BJMinduction}, if the answer to Question~\ref{que:theta5sq} is positive, then it would imply our Theorem~\ref{thm:h_6_sq} and a positive answer to Question~\ref{que:2theta6}. We plan to study these questions in a future project.\\
 173 
 174 As suggested by the proof of Corollary~\ref{cor:2line}, computation of differentials in the Adams spectral sequence has a long history. See Section 2 of \cite{WX61} for a brief summary at all primes and computations at the prime 2 up to around 60-stem. For more recent computations up to around 90-stem, see \cite{IWX, IWXsurvey, WXems, WXicm}.
 175 
 176 Many methods were introduced to compute differentials in the Adams spectral sequence. We highlight some of the major methods as follows.
 177 \begin{enumerate}
 178     \item Multiplicative structure of the Adams $E_2$-page and the Leibniz rule. \\
 179     
 180      \noindent By computing the Adams $E_2$-page with its multiplicative structure via the May spectral sequence, and comparing with Toda's unstable computations, May \cite{Maythesis} computed all differentials up to the 28-stem.\\
 181     
 182     \item Higher product structure -- interactions between Massey products and Toda brackets through Moss's theorem \cite{Moss}.\\
 183 
 184     \item The Mahowald trick \cite{MahowaldTangora}, which translates between differentials and extension problems.\\
 185 
 186     \noindent By using both Moss's theorem and the Mahowald trick, Barratt--Mahowald--Tangora \cite{MahowaldTangora, BarrattMahowaldTangora} computed all but one differentials up to the 45-stem; The remaining one was computed by Bruner \cite{Brunerdiff} using power operations.\\
 187 
 188     \item Comparison with the motivic Adams spectral sequence via the Betti realization functor \cite{DuggerIsaksen}.\\
 189 
 190     \noindent By comparison via the Betti realization functor, Isaksen \cite{Isaksen} gave rigorous arguments for all but one differentials in both the motivic and \emph{classical} Adams spectral sequence up to the 59-stem; The remaining one was computed by Xu \cite{IsaksenXu} using the higher Leibniz rule for motivic Massey products. \\
 191 
 192     \item Wang--Xu's $\mathbb{R}P^\infty$-method \cite{WX61}.\\
 193 
 194     \noindent Using Lin's algebraic Kahn--Priddy theorem \cite{LinalgKP}, Wang--Xu introduced a technique to prove Adams differentials inductively using differentials in subquotients of $\mathbb{R}P^\infty$, and completed computations of Adams differentials up to the 61-stem. (See also \cite{WangXu51ext} for application of this method on extension problems.) \\
 195 
 196     \noindent There is a nice geometric application of these differentials in stems 60 and 61. The sphere $S^{61}$ has a unique smooth structure and it is the last odd dimensional case: The only ones are $S^1, \ S^3, \ S^5$ and $S^{61}$.\\
 197 
 198     \item Gheorghe--Isaksen--Wang--Xu's motivic cofiber of tau method \cite{GWX, IWX}. \\
 199 
 200     \noindent By identifying the algebraicity of the special fiber of a motivic deformation, Gheorghe--Wang--Xu \cite{GWX} proved that the motivic Adams spectral sequence of the cofiber of tau is isomorphic to the algebraic Novikov spectral sequence for BP$_*$, which can be completely computed in a large range by Wang's program \cite{Wang}. Then differentials in the classical Adams spectral sequence follow from naturality.\\ 
 201     
 202     \noindent Using this method, together with some others, Isaksen--Wang--Xu \cite{IWX} computed Adams differentials up to the 90-stem, with only a few exceptions. \\
 203 
 204     \item $\HF$-synthetic/filtered spectra method \cite{Pst, Burklund, BurklundXu, BurklundIsaksenXu}.\\
 205 
 206     \noindent The homotopy ring of the $\HF$-synthetic sphere \cite{Pst, BHS} can be viewed as a tool to encode homotopical information of the classical Adams spectral sequence, in analog to the relation between the homotopy ring of the $\mathbb{C}$-motivic sphere \cite{Isaksen} and the Adams--Novikov spectral sequence. \\
 207 
 208     \noindent By studying the $\HF$-synthetic sphere, Burklund--Isaksen--Xu \cite{BurklundIsaksenXu} proved a few Adams differentials up to the 90-stem that were left out by Isaksen--Wang--Xu \cite{IWX}.\\
 209 
 210     
 211 \end{enumerate}
 212 
 213 Our strategy for proving our main Theorem \ref{thm:h_6_sq} that $h_6^2$ survives is three-fold. 
 214 
 215 \begin{itemize}
 216     \item Lin's Program.
 217 
 218     \noindent By establishing the theory of noncommutative Gr\"obner bases for Steenrod algebras \cite{LinGrobner}, Lin made computer programs that compute Ext groups in a very effective way. We use Lin's program to compute the Adams $E_2$-pages for a vast collection of finite spectra and the maps between them. For a detailed account of the range of Adams $E_2$ data computed, see \cite{LWXMachine}.\\
 219     
 220     \noindent Building on the theory of secondary Steenrod algebras \cite{BJ, Nassau, Chua}, we also leverage Lin’s program to compute Adams $d_2$-for certain finite spectra. Details on the $d_2$-differentials we have computed can also be found in \cite{LWXMachine}.\\
 221     
 222     \noindent Additionally, Lin’s program facilitates the propagation of differentials and extensions by employing the Leibniz rule and the naturality of Adams spectral sequences and extension spectral sequences (Definition \ref{def:ess}). \\
 223 
 224     \item The Generalized Leibniz Rule and the Generalized Mahowald Trick. \\
 225 
 226     \noindent The use of $\HF$-synthetic/filtered spectra allows us to discuss elements on the Adams $E_n$-page as classes in the homotopy groups of certain synthetic spectra \cite{Pst, BurklundXu}. In particular, naturality on synthetic homotopy groups allows us to discuss jump-of-filtration phenomena on any Adams $E_n$-page in a rigorous way. \\
 227     
 228     \noindent Using $\HF$-synthetic spectra, we develop the Generalized Leibniz Rule (Theorem~\ref{thm:e73f481e}) and the Generalized Mahowald Trick (Theorem~\ref{thm:158d451a}), which can be viewed as generalizations of the classical Mahowald's trick and geometric boundary theorem studies by Ravenel, Behrens and others \cite{RavenelGreenbook, Behrens, Ma, BurklundCookware}. We then use them to further propagate differentials from the known ones.\\
 229 
 230     \noindent With the Generalized Leibniz Rule and the Generalized Mahowald Trick, Lin’s program becomes even more powerful, enabling the derivation of additional differentials. The program rigorously verifies the conditions of the relevant theorems and generates human-readable proofs \cite{LWXZenodo} for all differentials computed by the machine. In the Appendix, readers will find tables of Adams differentials that are used in the proof of the main Theorem \ref{thm:h_6_sq}.\\
 231 
 232     \item The inductive approach and adhoc arguments near stem 126.\\
 233 
 234     \noindent By studying Barratt--Jones--Mahowald's inductive approach \cite{BJMinduction} in the $\HF$-synthetic context, Burklund--Xu (Proposition 7.19 of \cite{BurklundXu}) proved that $h_6^2$ is a permanent cycle in the classical Adams spectral sequence if and only if $\lambda \eta \theta_5^2 = 0$ in the homotopy group of the synthetic sphere. Here $\lambda$ is our notation for the synthetic deformation parameter, and $\theta_5$ is any synthetic homotopy class that is detected by $h_5^2$ on the Adams $E_2$-page.   \\
 235 
 236     \noindent There are 105 additive generators in stem 125 of the classical Adams $E_2$-page -- these are the potential targets that could be hit by a nonzero differential from $h_6^2$. Using Lin's program and the inductive approach, we can rule out 101 out of the 105 elements.\\
 237     
 238     \noindent By further applying the Generalized Leibniz Rule and the Generalized Mahowald Trick, we can reduce the possibility of a nonzero differential supported by $h_6^2$ to only one case: A nonzero $d_{12}$ hits a certain element in stem 125, filtration 14 (see Proposition~\ref{prop:possible_h_6_sq}(2)). A necessary condition for this nonzero $d_{12}$ is an $\eta$-extension from stem 124, filtration 10 to stem 125, filtration 14 (see Proposition~\ref{prop:possible_h_6_sq}(5)). \\
 239     
 240     \noindent By a careful inspection of the homotopy groups of the synthetic sphere (and on certain Adams $E_n$-pages) near stem 126, we use adhoc arguments to prove that the $\eta$-extension in the previous paragraph cannot happen (see Proposition~\ref{prop:state5false}) and conclude that $h_6^2$ survives in the Adams spectral sequence. \\
 241 \end{itemize} 
 242 
 243 \noindent\textbf{Organization.} \ In Section 2, we setup and discuss properties of an extension spectral sequence for a map $f: X\to Y$ between two spectra, whose $E_0$-page is isomorphic to the direct sum of $E_\infty$-pages of the Adams spectral sequences of $X$ and $Y$, and differentials correspond to $f_*: \pi_*X \to \pi_*Y$, filtered by the Adams filtration. This setup allows us to discuss the jump-of-filtration phenomena rigorously. In Section 3, we recall and discuss certain properties of $\HF$-synthetic spectra. In Section 4, we discuss the extension spectral sequence from Section 2 in the context of $\HF$-synthetic spectra. In Section 5, we discuss extensions on a classical Adams $E_n$-page, defined in terms of $\HF$-synthetic spectra. In Section 6, using the language of Sections 4 and 5, we develop the Generalized Leibniz Rule and the Generalized Mahowald Trick. In Section 7, we use the inductive approach, and ad hoc arguments prove that $h_6^2$ survives in the Adams spectral sequence. Necessary information of the classical Adams spectral sequence for the final ad hoc arguments is included in the Appendix.\\

 252 
 253 \section{A Spectral Sequence for Extensions}\label{sec:e6be737c}
 254 In this paper, we assume that all symbols $X,Y,Z,W,\dots$ for spectra represent 2-completed connective spectra of finite type. In particular, their $\HF$-Adams spectral sequences converge strongly.
 255 
 256 Consider a map $f: X\to Y$ between two spectra and we can construct the following chain complex
 257 $$0\to \pi_*X\fto{\pi_*f} \pi_*Y\to 0$$
 258 whose homology is 
 259 $$\Ker(\pi_*f)\oplus \Coker(\pi_*f).$$
 260 We encounter $f$-extension problems when analyzing the homotopy groups through the $E_\infty$-pages of the $\HF$-based Adams spectral sequences. To address these extensions, we introduce the following spectral sequence.
 261 
 262 
 263 
 264 \begin{definition}\label{def:ess}
 265   For a map $f: X\to Y$, we define an $f$-extension spectral sequence (denoted $f$-ESS) as the spectral sequence derived from filtering the above chain complex according to the Adams filtration on $\pi_*$. The $E_0$-page of this spectral sequence is isomorphic to the direct sum of $E_\infty$-pages of the Adams spectral sequences of $X$ and $Y$: 
 266   $$\ESS{f}_0^{s,t} \cong E_\infty^{s,t}(X) \oplus E_\infty^{s,t}(Y) \Longrightarrow \Ker(\pi_*f)\oplus \Coker(\pi_*f)$$
 267   where the $d_0$-differential is induced by $f$ on the $E_\infty$-pages.
 268   
 269   Differentials in this spectral sequence has the form
 270   $$d_n^{f}: \ESS{f}_n^{s,t}\to \ESS{f}_n^{s+n, t+n}.$$
 271 \end{definition}
 272 
 273 To prevent confusion between differentials $d_n$ in the Adams spectral sequence of a spectrum $X$ and those in the $f$-extension spectral sequence, we denote the latter with a superscript, writing them as $d_n^{f}$. 
 274 
 275 In the remainder of the paper, we denote by $\AF(f)$ the Adams filtration of a map $f$. Recall that $\AF(f) \ge k$ if and only if there exist $k$ composable maps $f_1, \ldots, f_k$, each inducing a trivial map in $\HF$-homology, such that $f$ is homotopic to the composition $f_k \circ \cdots \circ f_1$ (see \cite[Theorem~2.2.14]{RavenelGreenbook} for example).
 276 
 277 \begin{notation}\label{nota:6fb333a2}
 278     Let $\ZESS{f}^{s,t}_n(X)\subset E^{s,t}_\infty(X)$ be the subgroup consisting of elements for which the $f$-extension differentials $d^f_0,\dots,d^f_n$ vanish.
 279     Let $\BESS{f}^{s,t}_n(Y)\subset E^{s,t}_\infty(Y)$ be the subgroup generated by the sum of images of the $f$-extension differentials $d^f_0,\dots,d^f_n$. We define $\BESS{f}^{s,t}_{-1}(Y)=0$ and $\ZESS{f}^{s,t}_{-1}(X)=E^{s,t}_\infty(X)$.\\
 280     
 281     By definition we have
 282     $$\ESS{f}^{s,t}_n\iso \ZESS{f}^{s,t}_{n-1}(X)\oplus \big(E^{s,t}_\infty(Y)/\BESS{f}^{s,t}_{n-1}(Y)\big).$$
 283     
 284     A nonzero $d^f_n$ differential takes the form
 285     $$d^f_n: \ZESS{f}^{s,t}_{n-1}(X)\to E^{s+n,t+n}_\infty(Y)/\BESS{f}^{s+n,t+n}_{n-1}(Y).$$
 286      When we write 
 287     $$d^f_n(x)=y$$
 288     for $x\in E_\infty^{s,t}(X)$ and $y\in E_\infty^{s+n,t+n}(Y)$, it is understood that $x$ belongs to $\ZESS{f}^{s,t}_{n-1}(X)$, and 
 289     $$d^f_n(x)=y+\BESS{f}^{s+n,t+n}_{n-1}(Y).$$
 290     Furthermore, we may sometimes write
 291     $$d^f_n(x)\equiv y\mod M$$
 292     for some subgroup $M\subset E_\infty^{s+n,t+n}(Y)$. This means that 
 293     $$d^f_n(x)=y+m+\BESS{f}^{s+n,t+n}_{n-1}(Y)$$
 294     for some $m\in M$.
 295 \end{notation}
 296 
 297 \begin{definition}\label{def:768fba8a}
 298     We say there is an $f$-extension from $x\in E_\infty^{s,t}(X)$ to $y\in E_\infty^{s+n,t+n}(Y)$ if $d_n^{f}(x)=y$ in the $f$-ESS. We say this $f$-extension is $\textbf{essential}$ if $y$ is nontrivial in the $E_n$-page of the $f$-ESS, or equivalently $$y\notin \BESS{f}^{s+n,t+n}_{n-1}(Y).$$
 299     Otherwise we refer to it as inessential.
 300 \end{definition}
 301 
 302 \begin{notation}
 303     For $x\in E_\infty^{s,t}(X)$, we denote by $\{x\}$  the set of all classes in $\pi_{t-s}X$ that are detected by $x$. We use $[x]$ to refer to a specific class or a general class in $\{x\}$, with the choice understood from the context.
 304 \end{notation}
 305 
 306 
 307 \begin{proposition}\label{prop:8154e6f1}
 308 Consider $f: X\to Y$, $x\in E_\infty^{s,t}(X)$, $y\in E_\infty^{s+n,t+n}(Y)$ and $y'\in E_\infty^{s+m,t+m}(Y)$ for $m,n\ge 0$.
 309 \begin{enumerate}
 310     \item There is an $f$-extension from $x$ to $y$, i.e., $d_n^{f}(x)=y$ in the $f$-ESS if and only if there is a class $[x]\in \{x\}$ such that $f[x]\in \{y\}$.
 311     \item An $f$-extension from $x$ to $y$ is inessential, i.e., $y$ is trivial in the $E_n$-page of the $f$-ESS if and only if there exists an element $x'\in E_\infty^{s+a,t+a}(X)$ for $0<a\le n$ such that we have an essential differential $d_{n-a}^{f}(x')=y$. Equivalently, there exists a class $[x'] \in \{x'\} \subset \pi_*X$ with AF$(x')>$AF$(x)$, such that $f[x'] \in \{y\}$.
 312     
 313     \item Suppose we have an $f$-extension from $x$ to both $y$ and $y'$.
 314     \begin{enumerate}
 315         \item If $m=n$, then $y-y'\in \BESS{f}^{s+n,t+n}_{n-1}(Y)$ and there exists an element $x'\in E_\infty^{s+a,t+a}(X)$ for $0<a\le n$ such that we have an essential differential 
 316         $$d_{n-a}^{f}(x') = y-y'$$
 317         in $f$-ESS. Equivalently, there exists a class $[x'] \in \{x'\} \subset \pi_*X$ with AF$(x')>$AF$(x)$, such that $f[x'] \in \{y-y'\}$. 
 318         \item If $m>n$, then the $f$-extension from $x$ to $y$ is inessential.
 319      \end{enumerate}
 320 \end{enumerate}
 321 \end{proposition}
 322 
 323 \begin{proof}
 324     Part (1) is straightforward from the setup of the extension spectral sequence and Definition~\ref{def:768fba8a}. Parts (2) and (3) follow from Part (1).
 325 \end{proof}
 326 
 327 \begin{corollary}
 328     If the Adams filtration of $f$ is $k$, then
 329     $d^f_i=0$ for $i<k$.
 330 \end{corollary}
 331 
 332 \begin{proof}
 333     When $k>0$, it is clear that $d^f_0=0$, which means that for any $s$ and $[x]\in F_s\pi_*(X)$, we have $f([x])\in F_{s+1}\pi_*(Y)$. Since $\AF(f)=k$, we know that there exist $k$ maps
 334     $$X=X_0\fto{f_1}X_1\fto{f_2}\cdots\fto{f_k}X_k=Y$$
 335     such that each $f_i$ induces trivial map in $\HF$-homology (hence $\AF(f_i)>0$) and $f$ is homotopic to the composition $f_k\circ \cdots\circ f_1.$
 336     Then for any $s$ and $[x]\in F_s\pi_*(X)$, inductively we have
 337     $$f_1([x])\in F_{s+1}\pi_*(X_1)$$
 338     $$f_2\circ f_1([x])\in F_{s+2}\pi_*(X_2)$$
 339     $$\cdots$$
 340     $$f([x])=f_k\circ\cdots\circ f_1([x])\in F_{s+k}\pi_*(X_k)=F_{s+k}\pi_*(Y).$$
 341     By Proposition \ref{prop:8154e6f1}.(1) this means that $d_i^fx=0$ for any $x\in E_\infty^{*,*}(X)$ and $i<k$.
 342 \end{proof}
 343 
 344 \begin{definition} \label{def:98skj23}
 345 \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
 346     \item For $x\in E_\infty^{s,t}(X)$, we say that the an $f$-extension $d_n^{f}(x)=y$ has a crossing that hits Adams filtration $p$ for some $p \leq s+n$, if there exists an element $x^\prime\in E_\infty^{s+a,t+a}(X)$ with $a>0$ and an $f$-extension $d_m^{f}(x^\prime)=y^\prime$ for $0\neq y^\prime\in E^{s+a+m, t+a+m}_\infty(Y)$ such that
 347     $$p = \textup{AF}(y^\prime) = s+a+m\le \textup{AF}(y) = s+n.$$
 348     \item We say that an $f$-extension $d_n^{f}(x)=y$ has no crossing that hits the range AF $\ge p$ if there does not exist an element $x^\prime\in E_\infty^{s+a,t+a}(X)$ with $a>0$ and an $f$-extension $d_m^{f}(x^\prime)=y^\prime$ for $0\neq y^\prime\in E^{s+a+m, t+a+m}_\infty(Y)$ such that
 349     $$p\le \textup{AF}(y^\prime) = s+a+m\le \textup{AF}(y) = s+n.$$
 350     \item We say that an $f$-extension $d_n^{f}(x)=y$ has no crossing if it has no crossing that hits the range AF$\ge \AF(x) + 1$.
 351 \end{enumerate}
 352 \end{definition}

 394 \begin{remark}   
 395 
 396 The following statements are equivalent.
 397 \begin{itemize}
 398     \item An $f$-extension $d_n^{f}(x)=y$ has no crossing that hits the range AF$\ge p$. 
 399     \item If for any $a>0$ there is an $f$-extension from $x^\prime\in E_\infty^{s+a,t+a}(X)$ to some nontrivial $y^\prime$, then AF$(y^\prime)<p$ or AF$(y^\prime)>$AF$(y)$.
 400 \end{itemize} 
 401 \end{remark}
 402     
 403 \begin{remark}   
 404 We do not require the crossings in Definition~\ref{def:98skj23}~(1)(2) to be essential. A crossing implies the existence of an essential crossing, possibly a shorter one.
 405 \end{remark}
 406 
 407 
 408 \begin{proposition} \label{prop:i8r47oe}
 409     An $f$-extension from $x$ to $y$ has no crossing in the range $\AF\ge p$ if and only if for all $[x]\in \{x\}$ such that $\AF(f[x])\ge p$ we have $f[x]\in \{y\}$. In particular, we have
 410     \begin{enumerate}
 411         \item An $f$-extension from $x$ to $y$ has no crossing if and only if for all $[x]\in \{x\}$ we have $f[x]\in \{y\}$.
 412         \item An $f$-extension $d_n^{f}(x)=0$ has no crossing if and only if for all $[x]\in \{x\}$,
 413         $$\AF(f[x]) >\AF(x)+n.$$
 414         \item If $\AF(f)=n$, then all $d^f_n$-differentials have no crossing in the $f$-ESS.
 415     \end{enumerate}
 416 \end{proposition}
 417 
 418 \begin{proof}
 419     First we prove the only if part. Assume by contradiction that there exists $[x]\in \{x\}$ such that $f[x]=[y']\notin \{y\}$ is detected by $y'\in E_\infty(Y)$ with $\AF(y')\ge p$. Then there is an $f$ extension from $x$ to $y'$. By Proposition \ref{prop:8154e6f1}~(3), $y'$ (if $\AF(y)>\AF(y')$)  or $y-y'$ (if $\AF(y)=\AF(y')$) or $y$ (if $\AF(y)<\AF(y')$) should be hit by a shorter $d^f$ differential. This shorter differential is a crossing of the $f$-extension from $x$ to $y$ that hits $\AF\ge p$, which is a contradiction. Now we prove the if part. Assume that the $f$-extension has a crossing from $x'$ to $y'$ with $p\le \AF(y')\le \AF(y)$. Then there exists $[x']\in \{x'\}$ such that $f([x'])\in \{y'\}$. Note that $[x]+[x']\in \{x\}$ since $\AF(x')>\AF(x)$, we have
 420     $$f([x]+[x'])=f([x])+f([x'])=[y]+[y']\in\begin{cases}
 421         \{y'\} & \text{ if } \AF(y')<\AF(y)\\
 422         \{y+y'\} & \text{ if } \AF(y')=\AF(y).\\
 423     \end{cases}$$
 424     However, by assumption we should have $f([x]+[x'])\in \{y\}$ and it is contradictory to the equation above in either case.
 425     
 426     Hence the main statement is true.  Part (1) (2) are special cases of the main statement and Part (3) holds for degree reasons.
 427 \end{proof}
 428 
 429 
 430 \begin{example}
 431     Consider the Hopf map $\eta:S^1 \rightarrow S^0$ between 2-completed spheres. It is known that it induces a surjective map on $\pi_{46}$ (see [IWX] for example). 
 432     \begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1cm]
 433         \item In the $\eta$-ESS, we have the following nonzero differentials -- these are the essential $\eta$-extensions:
 434     \begin{enumerate}
 435         \item $d_1^\eta(h_5d_0) = h_1h_5d_0$ in AF$=6$,
 436         \item $d_2^\eta(\Delta h_1g) = d_0l$ in AF$=11$,
 437         \item $d_3^\eta(h_1g_2) = \Delta h_2c_1$ in AF$=8$,
 438         \item $d_4^\eta(h_3^2 h_5) = Mh_1$ in AF$=7$.\\
 439     \end{enumerate}
 440     
 441     \item The $\eta$-extension $d_2^\eta (h_0h_3^2h_5) = h_1h_5d_0$ is inessential.\\
 442     
 443         This is a zero differential on the $E_2$-page of the $\eta$-ESS due to the nonzero $d_1^\eta$-differential that hits $h_1h_5d_0$. Note that although this $\eta$-extension is inessential, we still have a class $[h_0h_3^2h_5] \in \{h_0h_3^2h_5\}$ such that $\eta \cdot [h_0h_3^2h_5] \in \{h_1h_5d_0\}$, due to the presence of an essential $\eta$-extension from $h_5d_0$ to $h_1h_5d_0$.\\
 444     
 445     \item The $\eta$-extension $d_4^\eta(h_3^2 h_5) = Mh_1$ has a crossing that hits AF$=6$.\\ 
 446     
 447     In fact, both the essential extension $d_1^\eta(h_5d_0) = h_1h_5d_0$ and the inessential extension $d_2^\eta (h_0h_3^2h_5) = h_1h_5d_0$ are such crossings. Due to the existence of a crossing, the following statement is NOT true:\\
 448 
 449     For all $[h_3^2 h_5] \in \{h_3^2 h_5\}$, we have $\eta \cdot [h_3^2 h_5] \in \{Mh_1\}$.\\
 450 
 451     In fact, there exists a class $[h_3^2 h_5] \in \{h_3^2 h_5\}$ such that $\eta \cdot [h_3^2 h_5] \in \{h_1h_5d_0\}$ due to the crossing $\eta$-extension from $h_5d_0$ to $h_1h_5d_0$.\\
 452 
 453     \item The $\eta$-extension $d_1^\eta(h_5d_0) = h_1h_5d_0$ has no crossing by part (3).
 454    
 455  \end{itemize}
 456 \end{example}

 461 \begin{theorem}\label{thm:4114f70c}
 462     Consider a homotopy commutative diagram of spectra
 463     $$\xymatrix{
 464     X \ar[r]^{f}\ar[d]_{p} & Y\ar[d]^{q}\\
 465     Z \ar[r]_{g} & W
 466     }$$
 467     Suppose $m,n,l\ge 0$, $0<k\le m+l-n$,
 468     $$\begin{array}{ll}
 469         x\in E^{s,t}_\infty(X) & y\in E^{s+n,t+n}_\infty(Y),\\
 470         z\in E^{s+m,t+m}_\infty(Z) & w\in E^{s+m+l,t+m+l}_\infty(W),
 471     \end{array}$$
 472     and
 473     \begin{enumerate}
 474         \item $d^{f}_{n}(x)=y$,
 475         \item $d^{p}_{m}(x)=z$,
 476         \item the differential in (1) or the differential in (2) has no crossing,
 477         \item $d^{g}_{l}(z)=w$, and has no crossing that hits the range AF $\ge s+n+k$,
 478         \item $d^{q}_{k-1} y=0$, and has no crossing.
 479     \end{enumerate}
 480     Then $d^{q}_{m+l-n}(y)=w$. (See Figure \ref{fig:4f154a8}.)
 481 \end{theorem}
 482 
 483 \begin{proof}
 484     First we find a representative $[x]$ of $x$ such that
 485     \begin{equation}\label{eq:9d45feac}
 486         p[x]\in \{z\}
 487     \end{equation}
 488     and
 489     \begin{equation}\label{eq:36e72616}
 490         f[x]\in \{y\}.
 491     \end{equation}
 492     If (1) has no crossing, by (2) we can pick $[x]\in \{x\}$ such that (\ref{eq:9d45feac}) holds;
 493     If (2) has no crossing, by (1) we can pick $[x]\in \{x\}$ such that  (\ref{eq:36e72616}) holds.
 494     In both cases the other equation also holds because of the no crossing condition.
 495     
 496     By (5), (\ref{eq:36e72616}) and Proposition~\ref{prop:i8r47oe}~(2) we know that $qf[x]$ has AF $\ge s+n+k$. Since the diagram on the Adams $E_\infty$-pages commutes, we have
 497     $$AF(gp[x])=AF(qf[x])\ge s+n+k.$$
 498     Combining with (4), (\ref{eq:9d45feac}) and Proposition~\ref{prop:i8r47oe} we have
 499     $$gp[x]\in \{w\}.$$
 500     Therefore, $qf[x]\in \{w\}$ and by (\ref{eq:36e72616}) there is a $q$-extension from $y$ to $w$.
 501 \end{proof}

 554 
 555 \begin{corollary} \label{cor:0012nik}
 556     Consider a homotopy commutative diagram of spectra
 557     $$\xymatrix{
 558     X \ar[r]^{f}\ar[d]_{p} & Y\ar[d]^{q}\\
 559     Z \ar[r]_{g} & W
 560     }$$
 561     Suppose $m,n,l\ge 0$, 
 562     $$\begin{array}{ll}
 563         x\in E^{s,t}_\infty(X) & y\in E^{s+n,t+n}_\infty(Y),\\
 564         z\in E^{s+m,t+m}_\infty(Z) & w\in E^{s+m+l,t+m+l}_\infty(W),
 565     \end{array}$$
 566     and
 567     \begin{enumerate}
 568         \item $d^{f}_{n}(x)=y$,
 569         \item $d^{p}_{m}(x)=z$,
 570         \item the differential in (1) or the differential in (2) has no crossing,
 571         \item $d^{g}_{l}(z)=w$, and has no crossing.
 572     \end{enumerate}
 573     Then $d^{q}_{m+l-n}(y)=w$.
 574 \end{corollary}
 575 
 576 \begin{corollary} \label{cor:166dc180}
 577     Consider a homotopy commutative diagram of spectra
 578     $$\xymatrix{
 579     X \ar[r]^{f}\ar[dr]_{p} & Y\ar[d]^{q}\\
 580     & Z
 581     }$$
 582     Suppose $n,m\ge 0$, 
 583     $$x\in E^{s,t}_\infty(X), ~y\in E^{s+n,t+n}_\infty(Y), ~z\in E^{s+m,t+m}_\infty(Z)$$
 584     and
 585     \begin{enumerate}
 586         \item $d^{f}_{n}(x)=y$,
 587         \item $d^{p}_{m}(x)=z$,
 588         \item the differential in (1) or the differential in (2) has no crossing.
 589     \end{enumerate}
 590     Then $d^{q}_{m-n}(y)=z$.
 591 \end{corollary}
 592 
 593 \begin{proof}
 594 This is a special case of Corollary \ref{cor:0012nik} when $Z=W$ and $g=id_Z$.
 595 \end{proof}
 596 
 597 \begin{corollary} \label{cor:290d35ce}
 598     Consider a homotopy commutative diagram of spectra
 599     $$\xymatrix{
 600     X \ar[d]_{p}\ar[dr]^{q} & \\
 601     Z\ar[r]_{g} & W
 602     }$$
 603     Suppose $n,m\ge 0$, 
 604     $$x\in E^{s,t}_\infty(X), ~z\in E^{s+m,t+m}_\infty(Y), ~w\in E^{s+m+l,t+m+l}_\infty(W)$$
 605     and
 606     \begin{enumerate}
 607         \item $d^{p}_{m}(x)=z$,
 608         \item $d^{g}_{l}(z)=w$, and has no crossing.
 609     \end{enumerate}
 610     Then $d^{q}_{m+l}(x)=w$.
 611 \end{corollary}
 612 
 613 \begin{proof}
 614 This is a special case of Corollary \ref{cor:0012nik} when $X=Y$ and $f=id_X$.
 615 \end{proof}
 616 
 617 \begin{corollary}\label{cor:e7b20ae2}
 618     Consider a homotopy commutative diagram of spectra
 619     $$\xymatrix{
 620     X \ar[r]^{f}\ar[d]_{p} & Y\ar[d]^{q}\\
 621     Z \ar[r]_{g} & W
 622     }.$$
 623     Assume $\ESS{p}_0^{*,*}=\ESS{p}_r^{*,*}$ and $\ESS{q}_0^{*,*}=\ESS{q}_r^{*,*}$ for some $r\ge 0$. Then 
 624     $$(d^p_r, d^q_r): E_\infty^{*,*}(X)\oplus E_\infty^{*,*}(Y)\to E_\infty^{*,*}(Z)\oplus E_\infty^{*,*}(W)$$
 625     induces a map from the $f$-ESS to the $g$-ESS.
 626 \end{corollary}
 627 
 628 \begin{proof}
 629     Since $\ESS{p}_0^{*,*}=\ESS{p}_r^{*,*}$ and $\ESS{q}_0^{*,*}=\ESS{q}_r^{*,*}$, we know that $d^p_r$, $d^q_r$ have no crossings. By Corollary \ref{cor:0012nik}, we have $d^g_0\circ d^p_r = d^q_r\circ d^f_0$ and therefore $(d^p_r, d^q_r)$ induces a map from $\ESS{f}_1^{*,*}$ to $\ESS{g}_1^{*,*}$. Inductively we can apply Corollary \ref{cor:0012nik} again and show that $(d^p_r, d^q_r)$ induces a map from $\ESS{f}_n^{*,*}$ to $\ESS{g}_n^{*,*}$ and $d^g_n\circ d^p_r = d^q_r\circ d^f_n$ for all $n$.
 630 \end{proof}
 631 
 632 \begin{corollary}\label{cor:aed3d1a4}
 633     If the composition $g\circ f$ of two maps $f:X\to Y$, $g:Y\to Z$ is trivial, and $d_n^f(x)=y$ for $x\in E_\infty^{s,t}(X)$ and $y\in E_\infty^{s+n,t+n}(Y)$, then $y$ is a permanent cycle in the $g$-ESS, i.e., we have
 634     $d^g_m(y)=0$ for all $m\ge 0$.
 635 \end{corollary}
 636 
 637 \begin{proof}
 638     This follows immediately from the following commutative diagram
 639     $$\xymatrix{
 640     X \ar[r]^{f}\ar[d] & Y\ar[d]^{g}\\
 641     0 \ar[r] & Z
 642     }$$
 643     and Corollary \ref{cor:0012nik}.
 644 
 645     There is a second short proof using homotopy groups. By Proposition \ref{prop:8154e6f1}, we know that there exists $[x]\in \{x\}$ such that $f([x])\in \{y\}$. Let $[y]=f([x])\in \{y\}$ and we have $g([y])=g(f([x]))=0$. Hence $y$ is a permanent $d^g$ cycle.
 646 \end{proof}
 647 
 648 \begin{proposition}\label{prop:cfe810af}
 649     Suppose that the sequence of spectra
 650     $$X\fto{f}Y\fto{g}Z$$
 651     induces a sequence of homotopy groups
 652     $$\pi_*X\fto{\pi_*f}\pi_*Y\fto{\pi_*g}\pi_*Z$$
 653     that is exact in $\pi_*Y$. Then all permanent cycles in $E^{*,*}_\infty(Y)$ of the $g$-ESS are boundaries in the $f$-ESS.
 654 \end{proposition}
 655 
 656 \begin{proof}
 657     If we consider the following sequence
 658     \begin{equation}\label{eq:8c7eec14}
 659         0\to \pi_*X\fto{\pi_*f}\pi_*Y\fto{\pi_*g}\pi_*Z\to 0
 660     \end{equation}
 661     and treat it as a chain complex filtered by the Adams filtration, we obtain an extension spectral sequence comprising $d^f$ and $d^g$ differentials. The abutment of this spectral sequence, when projected onto the $Y$ component, is zero. Therefor, all permanent $d^g$-cycles are killed by $d^f$-differentials.
 662 
 663     Again we offer another proof via homotopy groups. Assume that $y\in E^{*,*}_\infty(Y)$ is a permanent $d^g$ cycle. By the convergence of ESS we know that it detects an $g$-torsion $[y]\in \{y\}$ with $g([y])=0$. Hence there exists $[x]\in \pi_*X$ such that $f([x])=[y]$ by exactness. Assume that $[x]$ is detected by $x\in E^{*,*}_\infty(X)$. Then there exists an $f$ extension from $x$ to $y$.
 664 \end{proof}
 665 
 666 Corollary \ref{cor:aed3d1a4} and Proposition \ref{prop:cfe810af} are particularly useful because they provide numerous potential applications whenever we have a cofiber sequence of the form
 667 $$X\fto{f}Y\fto{g}Cf\fto{h}\Sus X$$
 668 These results can also be implemented in a computer program to facilitate their application.
 669

 750 
 751 In Section~\ref{sec:e6be737c}, we explored classical extension problems in the context of commutative diagrams and cofiber sequences of spectra, framed in terms of the extension spectral sequence. The power of the extension spectral sequence can be further enhanced by generalizing and applying it to the category of $\HF$-synthetic spectra, a topic we will discuss in Section~\ref{sec:synext}.
 752 
 753 In this section, we recall some recent work of Pstragowski \cite{Pst} and Appendix A of Burklund--Hahn--Senger \cite{BHS} on $E$-synthetic spectra, which is strongly motivated by the work of Hu--Kriz--Ormsby \cite{HuKrizOrmsby}, Isaksen \cite{Isaksen}, Gheorghe--Wang--Xu \cite{GWX}, and Gheorghe--Isaksen--Krause--Ricka \cite{GIKR} on the $\mathbb{C}$-motivic spectra. We will specialize some of these results to $\HF$-synthetic spectra, refining and highlighting specific properties.
 754 
 755 The stable, presentable, symmetric monoidal $\infty$-category $\mathrm{Syn}_{\HF}$ of $\HF$-synthetic spectra is constructed in \cite{Pst}, along with a functor
 756 $$\nu: \mathrm{Sp}\to \mathrm{Syn}_{\HF},$$
 757 from the $\infty$-category of spectra $\mathrm{Sp}$. This functor is lax symmetric monoidal and preserves filtered colimits. However, $\nu$ does not generally preserve cofiber sequences. Instead, the following holds.
 758 
 759 \begin{proposition}[{\cite[Lemma 4.23]{Pst}}]\label{prop:1f7950df}
 760     Suppose that
 761     $$X\fto{f} Y\fto{g} Z$$
 762     is a cofiber sequence of spectra. Then
 763     $$\nu X\fto{\nu f}\nu Y\fto{\nu g} \nu Z$$
 764     is a cofiber sequence of ${\HF}$-synthetic spectra if and only if
 765     $$0\to {\HF}_*X\fto{{\HF}_*f} {\HF}_*Y\fto{{\HF}_*g} {\HF}_*Z\to 0$$
 766     is a short exact sequence.
 767 \end{proposition}
 768 
 769 
 770 \begin{definition}[{\cite[Definition 4.6]{Pst}}]
 771 The bigraded spheres $S^{s,w}$ are the synthetic spectra defined by $\Sigma^{s-w}\nu (S^w)$.
 772 \end{definition}
 773 
 774 \begin{definition}[{\cite[Definition 4.27]{Pst}}]
 775     The natural comparison map
 776     $$S^{0,-1}=\Sus \nu (S^{-1})\to \nu(\Sus S^{-1})=S^{0,0}$$
 777     in the category of $\HF$-synthetic spectra is denoted by $\lambda\in \pi_{0,-1}S^{0,0}$. We denote by $S^{0,0}/\lambda$ the cofiber of $\lambda$. In general for synthetic $X$ the following
 778     $$\lambda\wedge id_X: \Sus^{0,-1}X\to X.$$
 779     is a natural transformation and by abuse of notation this map is also denoted by $\lambda$.
 780     We denote by $X/\lambda$ the cofiber of $\lambda$
 781     which is equivalent to $(S^{0,0}/\lambda) \wedge X$.
 782 \end{definition}
 783 
 784 \begin{remark}
 785 For every $n\ge 1$, $S^{0,0}/\lambda^n$ is an $E_\infty$-object in $\mathrm{Syn}_{\HF}$ (see \cite[Appendix B, C]{BHSmot} for example).
 786 \end{remark}
 787 
 788 \begin{remark} \label{rem:deformation}
 789 The element $\lambda$ can be viewed as a parameter of a categorical deformation on $\mathrm{Syn}_{\HF}$ (see \cite{Pst}). 
 790 $$\xymatrix{\textup{Sp}^\wedge_2 & & \mathrm{Syn}_{\HF}\ar[ll]_-{\lambda^{-1}}^-{\textup{generic fiber}} \ar[rr]^-{mod ~  \lambda}_-{\textup{special fiber}} & & \mathcal{D}(A_*\textup{-Comod})}$$
 791 Inverting $\lambda$, the generic fiber is equivalent to the classical category of 2-completed spectra $\textup{Sp}^\wedge_2$. Modding out by $\lambda$, the special fiber is equivalent to Hovey's derived category \cite{Hovey} of comodules over the mod 2 dual Steenrod algebra $\mathcal{D}(A_*\textup{-Comod})$. Historically, this synthetic deformation is motivated by, and analogous to, the motivic deformation conjectured by Isaksen and proven by Gheorghe--Wang--Xu \cite{GWX}: Specifically, there exists a map
 792 $$\tau: \widehat{S^{0,-1}} \rightarrow \widehat{S^{0,0}}$$
 793 between p-completed motivic spheres. This element $\tau$
 794 can be viewed as a parameter of a categorical deformation on the category of cellular objects over $\widehat{S^{0,0}}$. 
 795 $$\xymatrix{\textup{Sp}^\wedge_p & & \widehat{S^{0,0}}\textup{-Mod}\ar[ll]_-{\tau^{-1}}^-{\textup{generic fiber}} \ar[rr]^-{mod ~  \tau}_-{\textup{special fiber}} & & \mathcal{D}(BP_*BP\textup{-Comod})}$$
 796 Inverting $\tau$, the generic fiber is equivalent to the classical category of p-completed spectra $\textup{Sp}^\wedge_p$. Modding out by $\tau$, the special fiber is equivalent to Hovey's derived category of comodules over the Hopf algebroid $BP_*BP$. 
 797 \end{remark}
 798 
 799 \begin{theorem}[{\cite[Theorem A.8]{BHS}}] \label{thm:rigid}
 800     The synthetic Adams spectral sequence for $\nu X$
 801      $$\SynSS_2^{s,t,w}(\nu X)\Longrightarrow \pi_{t-s,w}(\nu X)$$
 802      has an $E_2$-page 
 803     $$\SynSS_2^{*,*,*}(\nu X) \cong E_2^{*,*}(X)\otimes \mathbb{F}_2[\lambda]$$
 804     with differentials
 805     $$d_r: \SynSS_r^{s,t,w}\to \SynSS_r^{s+r,t+r-1,w}.$$
 806     Here $E_2^{*,*}(X) \cong \Ext_A^{*,*}(X)$ is the classical Adams $E_2$-page of $X$. An element in $E_2^{s,t}(X)$ is viewed as having tridegree $(s, t, t)$ in $\SynSS_2^{s,t,t}(\nu X)$, and $\lambda$ is in tridegree $(0, 0, -1)$. Given a classical Adams differential $d_r^{\mathrm{cl}}(x)=y$, the corresponding synthetic differential is $d_r(x)=\lambda^{r-1}y$, which is $\lambda$-linear, and all synthetic Adams differentials arise in this way.
 807 \end{theorem}
 808 
 809 \begin{remark}
 810     Here, we use a third grading of the synthetic Adams $E_2$-page that differs from \cite{BHS} but more compatible with \cite{BurklundIsaksenXu}. Specifically, we view elements in $E_2^{s,t}(X)$ as having tridegree $(s,t,t)$, instead of $(s,t,s)$ as in \cite{BHS}. This choice ensures that differentials and maps preserve the third degree, which proves convenient for the extension spectral sequence in the $\HF$-synthetic category in Section~\ref{sec:synext}.
 811 \end{remark}
 812 
 813 \begin{theorem}[{\cite[Theorem A.1]{BHS}}]\label{thm:17e90ac0}
 814     The synthetic Adams spectral sequence for $\nu X$ is isomorphic to the $\lambda$-Bockstein spectral sequence (with no sign difference because we are working over $\bF_2$ here). More specifically, we have
 815     \begin{enumerate}
 816         \item $E_2^{s,t}(X)\iso \pi_{t-s,t}(\nu X /\lambda)$.
 817         \item If we have a classical Adams differential $d_rx=y$ for 
 818         $$x\in E_2^{s,t}(X)\iso \pi_{t-s,t}(\nu X /\lambda),$$
 819         then $x$ admits a lift to $\pi_{t-s,t}(\nu X/\lambda^{r-1})$ whose image under the $\tau$-Bockstein
 820         $$\nu X/\lambda^{r-1}\to \Sus^{1,-r+1}\nu X/\lambda$$
 821         is equal to $d_r(x)$.
 822     \end{enumerate}
 823 \end{theorem}
 824 
 825 \begin{remark}
 826     The isomorphism with the $\lambda$-Bockstein spectral sequence in Theorem~\ref{thm:17e90ac0} and the correspondence of differentials between the classical and synthetic Adams spectral sequences in Theorem~\ref{thm:rigid} can be interpreted as manifestations of the rigidity of the synthetic Adams spectral sequence. This type of rigidity was first observed by Hu--Kriz--Ormsby \cite{HuKrizOrmsby} and Isaksen \cite{Isaksen} in the context of the motivic Adams--Novikov spectral sequence, and it was utilized extensively in the work of Gheorghe--Wang--Xu \cite{GWX}. Subsequently, Burklund--Xu \cite{BurklundXu} uncovered the rigidity of the motivic Cartan--Eilenberg spectral sequence and performed computations based on this property. See also \cite{mmf}, \cite{Burklund} for applications of this type of rigidity.
 827 
 828     Together with Remark~\ref{rem:deformation}, $\HF$-synthetic spectra can be seen as a categorification of the classical Adams spectral sequence, just as cellular motivic spectra can be viewed a categorification of the classical Adams--Novikov spectral sequence.
 829 \end{remark}
 830 
 831 \begin{notation}
 832     Consider the classical Adams spectral sequence of $X$. Let $B_r^{s,t}(X)$ denote the subgroups of $E_2^{s,t}(X)$ generated by the sum of images of Adams differentials $d_2,\dots,d_r$. Let $Z_r^{s,t}(X)$ denote the subgroup of $E_2^{s,t}(X)$ consisting of elements on which $d_2,\dots,d_r$ vanish. For $r=1$, we define $Z_1^{s,t}(X)=E_2^{s,t}(X)$ and $B_1^{s,t}(X)=0$. For $r=\infty$, let $Z_\infty^{s,t}(X)$ be the intersection of all $Z_r^{s,t}(X)$, and $B_\infty^{s,t}(X)$ be the union of all $B_r^{s,t}(X)$. We then have the following inclusions
 833     $$0 = B_1 \subset B_2\subset B_3\subset \cdots\subset B_\infty\subset Z_\infty \subset \cdots\subset Z_3\subset Z_2\subset Z_1 = E_2.$$
 834     In this article, we interpret a classical Adams differential $d_r$ as the map
 835     $$d_r: Z_{r-1}^{s,t}(X)\to E_2^{s+r,t+r-1}(X)/B_{r-1}^{s+r,t+r-1}(X),$$
 836     and follow the pattern in Notation \ref{nota:6fb333a2} for writing differentials.
 837 \end{notation}
 838 
 839 The following two propositions are taken from \cite[Corollary A.9, A.11]{BHS}. Note that our third grading, $w$, differs from that used in the reference.
 840 
 841 \begin{proposition}\label{prop:30e8b746}
 842     The $E_\infty$-page of the synthetic Adams spectral sequence for $\nu X$ is given by
 843     $$E_\infty^{s,t,w}(\nu X)\iso\begin{cases}
 844         Z_\infty^{s,t}(X)/B_{1+t-w}^{s,t}(X) & \text{ if } t \ge w,\\
 845         0 & \text{ otherwise }.
 846     \end{cases}$$
 847 \end{proposition}
 848 
 849 \begin{proposition}\label{prop:59f111f}
 850     The $E_\infty$-page of the synthetic Adams spectral sequence for $\nu X/\lambda^r$ for $r\ge 2$ is given by
 851     $$E_\infty^{s,t,w}(\nu X/\lambda^r)\iso\begin{cases}
 852         Z_{r-t+w}^{s,t}(X)/B_{1+t-w}^{s,t}(X) & \text{if } 0\le t-w < r,\\
 853         0 & \text{otherwise}.
 854     \end{cases}$$
 855 \end{proposition}
 856 
 857 \begin{notation}
 858     For convenience, whenever $n\le 0$ or $m\le 0$, we will define $Z_n(X)/B_m(X)$ as a trivial group. This is a bit counterintuitive to the definition of $Z_n$ and $B_m$ but it allows us to consistently express the following
 859     $$E_\infty^{s,t,w}(\nu X)\iso Z_\infty^{s,t}(X)/B_{1+t-w}^{s,t}(X)$$
 860     and
 861     $$E_\infty^{s,t,w}(\nu X/\lambda^r)\iso Z_{r-t+w}^{s,t}(X)/B_{1+t-w}^{s,t}(X).$$
 862 \end{notation}
 863 
 864 
 865 \begin{remark}
 866     The isomorphisms in Propositions~\ref{prop:30e8b746} and \ref{prop:59f111f} are compatible with the synthetic maps $\lambda^{r'-r}: \Sus^{-r'+r}\nu X/\lambda^r\to \nu X/\lambda^{r'}$ and $\rho: \nu X/\lambda^r\to \nu X/\lambda^{r''}$ for $r'\ge r\ge r''$. The induced map
 867     $$\lambda^{r'-r}:E_\infty^{s,t,w}(\nu X/\lambda^r)\to E_\infty^{s,t,w-r'+r}(\nu X/\lambda^{r'})$$
 868     corresponds to the quotient map (when all subscripts are positive):
 869     $$\lambda^{r'-r}:Z_{r-t+w}^{s,t}(X)/B_{1+t-w}^{s,t}(X)\to Z_{r-t+w}^{s,t}(X)/B_{1+t-w+r'-r}^{s,t}(X)$$
 870     Similarly, the induced map
 871     $$\rho:E_\infty^{s,t,w}(\nu X/\lambda^r)\to E_\infty^{s,t,w}(\nu X/\lambda^{r''})$$
 872     corresponds to the embedding map (when all subscripts are positive):
 873     $$\rho:Z_{r-t+w}^{s,t}(X)/B_{1+t-w}^{s,t}(X)\to Z_{r''-t+w}^{s,t}(X)/B_{1+t-w}^{s,t}(X).$$
 874     These data do not extend beyond classical information. However, when we consider a map $f: X\to Y$, we can obtain interesting extensions, as will be discussed in Section~\ref{sec:synext}.
 875 \end{remark}
 876 
 877 
 878 \begin{example} \label{exam:synEinfty}
 879     Consider the first few nonzero differentials in the classical Adams spectral sequence from the 15-stem to the 14-stem: 
 880     $$d_2(h_4) = h_0h_3^2,  \ d_3(h_0h_4) = h_0d_0, \ d_3(h_0^2h_4) = h_0^2d_0,$$
 881     where $$h_0 \in \Ext_A^{1,1}, \ h_3 \in \Ext_A^{1,8}, \ h_4 \in \Ext_A^{1,16}, \ d_0 \in \Ext_A^{4,18}.$$
 882     The $E_2$-page of the synthetic Adams spectral sequence for $S^{0,0}$ has the form
 883     $$\SynSS_2^{*,*,*}(S^{0,0}) \cong \Ext_A^{*,*}\otimes \mathbb{F}_2[\lambda],$$
 884     and we have
 885     $$h_0 \in \SynSS_2^{1,1,1}, \ h_3 \in \SynSS_2^{1,8,8}, \ h_4 \in \SynSS_2^{1,16,16}, \ d_0 \in \SynSS_2^{4,18,18}.$$
 886     The corresponding nonzero synthetic Adams differentials for $S^{0,0}$ are
 887     $$d_2(h_4) = \lambda h_0h_3^2,  \ d_3(h_0h_4) = \lambda^2 h_0d_0, \ d_3(h_0^2h_4) = \lambda^2 h_0^2d_0,$$
 888     along with their $\lambda$-multiples. As a result, the 14-stem of the synthetic $E_\infty$-page is generated by $\lambda$-free elements $h_3^2$ and $d_0$, along with the following $\lambda$-torsion elements:
 889     $$h_0h_3^2, \ h_0d_0, \ \lambda h_0d_0, \ h_0^2d_0, \ \lambda h_0^2d_0.$$
 890     On the other hand, since $h_0h_3^2 \in \Ext_A^{3,17}$ is a $d_2$-cycle, we have 
 891     $$0 = B_1^{3,17} \subset B_2^{3,17} = Z_\infty^{3,17} = E_2^{3,17} \cong \mathbb{F}_2\{h_0h_3^2\},$$
 892     which matches the description:
 893     $$E_\infty^{3,17,w}(S^{0,0})\iso\begin{cases}
 894         Z_\infty^{3,17}/B_{18-w}^{3,17} \cong \mathbb{F}_2\{h_0h_3^2\} & \text{ if } w = 17,\\
 895         Z_\infty^{3,17}/B_{18-w}^{3,17} = 0 & \text{ if } w \le 16,\\
 896         0 & \text{ if } w \ge 18.
 897     \end{cases}$$
 898     The element $h_0h_3^2$ detects homotopy classes of $\AF=3$ in $\pi_{14,17}$. Due to the presence of the $\lambda$-free element $d_0$ in the synthetic $E_\infty$-page, the element $\lambda d_0$ also detects homotopy classes in $\pi_{14,17}$, but with $\AF=4$. This illustrates why it is more convenient to describe the $E_\infty$-page as in Proposition~\ref{prop:30e8b746}, rather than in terms of synthetic homotopy groups.
 899     
 900     As a comparison, the only nonzero synthetic Adams differential in the 14-stem for $S^{0,0}/\lambda^2$ is 
 901     $$d_2(h_4) = \lambda h_0h_3^2,$$
 902     and the elements $\lambda h_4, \ h_0h_4, \ h_0^2h_4$ are all permanent cycles. 
 903 
 904     For $S^{0,0}/\lambda^3$, the nonzero synthetic Adams differentials in the 14-stem are
 905     $$d_2(h_4) = \lambda h_0h_3^2, \  d_2(\lambda h_4) = \lambda^2 h_0h_3^2,$$ 
 906     $$d_3(h_0h_4) = \lambda^2 h_0d_0, \ d_3(h_0^2h_4) = \lambda^2 h_0^2d_0,$$
 907     and the elements $\lambda^2 h_4, \ \lambda h_0h_4, \ \lambda^2 h_0h_4, \ \lambda h_0^2h_4, \lambda^2 h_0^2h_4$ are all permanent cycles. 
 908 
 909     These results can be compared with the statements in Proposition~\ref{prop:59f111f}.
 910 \end{example}
 911 
 912 \begin{proposition}[{\cite[Lemma 9.15]{BHS}}]\label{prop:ef21f9bc}
 913     If a map $f:X\to Y$ has Adams filtration $\AF(f)=k$, then there exists a factorization
 914     $$\xymatrix{
 915      & \Sus^{0,-k}\nu Y\ar[d]^{\lambda^k}\\
 916      \nu X \ar[r]_{\nu f}\ar@{-->}[ru]^{\Sus^{0,-k}\tilde f} & \nu Y
 917     }$$
 918     where $\tilde f: \Sus^{0,k}\nu X\to \nu Y$ is called a synthetic lift of $f$.
 919 \end{proposition}
 920 
 921 In Proposition \ref{prop:ef21f9bc} the map $\nu f$ can be also factorized as follows:
 922 $$\xymatrix{
 923  \nu X \ar[r]^{\nu f}\ar[d]_{\lambda^k} & \nu Y\\
 924  \Sus^{0,k}\nu X\ar@{-->}[ru]_{\tilde f}
 925 }$$
 926 
 927 %Proposition \ref{prop:1f7950df} and Proposition \ref{prop:ef21f9bc} are specializations of the original statements about general $E$-synthetic spectra to the case $E=\HF$. 
 928 
 929 \begin{proposition}\label{prop:41561db2}
 930     Suppose that we have a  distinguished triangle of spectra
 931     $$X\fto{f} Y\fto{g} Z\fto{h} \Sus X$$
 932     with $\AF(h)>0$, and consequently a short exact sequence on $\HF$-homology
 933     $$0\to H_*X\fto{H_*f} H_*Y\fto{H_*g} H_*Z\to 0.$$
 934     Then there exists a distinguished triangle of synthetic spectra
 935     $$\nu X\fto{\nu f}\nu Y\fto{\nu g} \nu Z\fto{\Sus^{0,-1}\hat h} \Sus^{0,-1}\nu \Sus X=\Sus^{1,0}\nu X$$
 936     such that $\nu h=\lambda \hat h$.
 937 \end{proposition}
 938 \begin{proof}
 939     The proof is contained in the proof of \cite[Lemma 9.15]{BHS}.
 940 \end{proof}
 941 
 942 \begin{remark}
 943 For $h$ in Proposition \ref{prop:41561db2}, let $\tilde h$ be a synthetic map such that $\nu h=\lambda^k\tilde h$, then $\hat h$ is equal to $\lambda^{k-1}\tilde h$ up to some $\lambda$-torsion. 
 944 \end{remark}
 945 
 946 \begin{notation} \label{not:fhat}
 947      For a map $f: X\to Y$ which is part of a distinguished triangle
 948     $$X\fto{f} Y\fto{g}Cf\to\Sus X,$$
 949     we define
 950     $$e(f)=\begin{cases}
 951      0 & \text{ if }\AF(f)=0,\\
 952      1 & \text{ if }\AF(f)>0.
 953     \end{cases}$$
 954     When $\AF(f)=0$, we also denote $\nu f$ by $\hat f$. In both cases, we have 
 955     $$\hat f: \Sus^{0,e(f)}\nu X\to \nu Y$$
 956     and $\nu f=\lambda^{e(f)}\hat f$. Further more, $C\hat f\simeq \Sus^{0,-e(g)}\nu Cf$ or equivalently,
 957     \begin{itemize}
 958         \item if $f$ induces a trivial map or an injection on $\HF$-homology, then $C\hat f\simeq \nu Cf$;
 959         \item if $f$ induces a surjection on $\HF$-homology, then $C\hat f \simeq \Sus^{0,-1}\nu Cf$.
 960     \end{itemize}
 961 \end{notation}
 962 
 963 With the notation above, we can rewrite Proposition \ref{prop:41561db2} into the following form which will be used in Theorem \ref{thm:158d451a} and help us unify different cases.
 964 
 965 \begin{proposition}
 966     Suppose that we have a distinguished triangle of spectra
 967     $$X\fto{f} Y\fto{g} Z\fto{h} \Sus X$$
 968     such that $e(f)+e(g)+e(h)=1$. Then we have a distinguished triangle of synthetic spectra
 969     $$\nu X\fto{\hat f} \Sus^{0,-e(f)}\nu Y\fto{\hat g} \Sus^{0,-e(f)-e(g)}\nu Z\fto{\hat h} \Sus^{0,-1}\nu \Sus X$$
 970     (For simplicity we often omit the suspensions before maps but keep the suspensions before spectra.)
 971 \end{proposition}
 972 
 973 \begin{remark}
 974     When $\AF(f)>1$, it is often more advantageous to work with $C\tilde f$ rather than $C\hat f$, as the $\lambda$-Bockstein spectral sequence for $C\tilde f$ corresponds to a modified Adams spectral sequence, which differs from the classical one but may provide additional information. On the other hand, given the equivalence  $C\hat{f}\simeq \nu Cf$ from Notation~\ref{not:fhat}, the rigidity Theorems~\ref{thm:rigid} and \ref{thm:17e90ac0} imply that the synthetic Adams spectral sequence for $C\hat f$ is isomorphic to the $\lambda$-Bockstein spectral sequence, and aligns with the classical Adams spectral sequence for $Cf$.  Since the computational data used in this work is based on the classical Adams spectral sequence, we defer the exploration of $C\tilde f$ to future work.
 975     
 976     To illustrate this in more detail, consider the example of the synthetic lift $\tilde{\eta^3} \in \pi_{3,6}$ of $\eta^3 \in \pi_3$, which has $\AF = 3$. We can examine several $\lambda$-multiples of $\tilde{\eta^3}$:
 977     $$\lambda \tilde{\eta^3} \in \pi_{3,5}, \ \ \hat{\eta^3} = \lambda^2 \tilde{\eta^3} \in \pi_{3,4}, \ \ \nu(\eta^3) = \lambda^3 \tilde{\eta^3} \in \pi_{3,3}.$$
 978 
 979    Now, consider the synthetic 2-cell complexes formed by taking the cofibers of these maps:
 980     $$C(\tilde{\eta^3}), \ C(\lambda\tilde{\eta^3}), \ C(\lambda^2\tilde{\eta^3}) \simeq C(\hat{\eta^3}) \simeq \nu (C\eta^3), \ C(\lambda^3\tilde{\eta^3}) \simeq C\nu(\eta^3).$$
 981 
 982     The rigidity Theorems~\ref{thm:rigid} and \ref{thm:17e90ac0} apply only to $C(\hat{\eta^3})$, as it is equivalent to $\nu (C\eta^3)$. In this case, its synthetic Adams $E_2$-page is $\lambda$-free over $\Ext_A^{*,*}(C\eta^3)$; in particular the identity element from the top cell has tridegree $(s,t,w) = (0,4,4)$. There is a correspondence of differentials between the classical and synthetic Adams spectral sequence, and a synthetic differential generates $\lambda$-torsion corresponding to its length.
 983 
 984     For $C(\tilde{\eta^3}), C(\lambda\tilde{\eta^3})$ and $C(\lambda^3\tilde{\eta^3})$, their synthetic Adams $E_2$-pages remain $\lambda$-free, but the identity element from the top cell has tridegree 
 985     $$(s,t,w) =(0,4,6), (0,4,5), (0,4,7)$$ 
 986     respectively. As a result, each supports a nonzero Adams $d_3$-differential killing $h_1^3$ from the bottom cell when multiplied by $1, \lambda, \lambda^3$, respectively. This leaves no $\lambda$-torsion, $\lambda^1$-torsion and $\lambda^3$-torsion in $\pi_{4,6}$, respectively.
 987 
 988     On the other hand, the $\lambda$-Bockstein spectral sequences for $C(\tilde{\eta^3})$ and $ C(\lambda\tilde{\eta^3})$ correspond to a modified Adams spectral sequence in the sense of \cite[Section 3]{BHHM}, with the AF of $\eta^3$ ``modified" to $\AF=1$ and $\AF=2$, respectively. In particular, the $E_2$-page of the $\lambda$-Bockstein spectral sequence for $C(\tilde{\eta^3})$, i.e., $\pi_{*,*}C(\tilde{\eta^3})/\lambda$, may contain more multiplicative information than $\Ext_A^{*,*}(C\eta^3)$. 
 989     
 990     
 991  %   For the synthetic Adams $E_2$-page of $C(\lambda\tilde{\eta^3})$, it remains $\lambda$-free, but the contributions from the top cell will increase their AF by 1, and the first nonzero Adams differential is a $d_2$. As for $C(\tilde{\eta^3})$, its synthetic Adams $E_2$-page is also $\lambda$-free, and the contributions from the top cell will increase their AF by 2. In particular, $h_1^3$ from the bottom cell is already zero on the $E_2$-page, as if it were killed by a $d_1$. 
 992     
 993  %   In the other direction, for $C(\lambda^3\tilde{\eta^3}) \simeq C\nu(\eta^3)$, its synthetic Adams $E_2$-page is no longer $\lambda$-free, and the contributions from the top cell will lower their AF by 1, leaving $h_1^3$ from the bottom cell as a $\lambda^3$-torsion on the $E_2$-page, as if it were killed by a $d_4$.
 994     
 995    
 996 \end{remark}
 997 
 998 \section{Synthetic Extensions} \label{sec:synext}
 999 The extension spectral sequence introduced in Section \ref{sec:e6be737c} can be generalized for $\HF$-synthetic spectra. Consider a map $f: X\to Y$ between two connective finite $\HF$-synthetic spectra\footnote{In our solution to the Kervaire invariant problem, we only consider finite synthetic spectra. However, the arguments in this section apply to any synthetic spectra for which the synthetic Adams spectral sequence strongly converges.}. By filtering
1000 $$0\to \pi_{*,*}X\fto{\pi_{*,*}f} \pi_{*,*}Y\to 0$$
1001 using the Adams filtration, we obtain an $f$-extension spectral sequence:
1002 $$\ESS{f}_0^{s,t,w} \cong E_\infty^{s,t,w}(X) \oplus E_\infty^{s,t,w}(Y) \Longrightarrow \Ker(\pi_{*,*}f)\oplus \Coker(\pi_{*,*}f)$$
1003 with differential
1004 $$d_n^f: \ZESS{f}_{n-1}^{s,t,w}(X)\to E_\infty^{s+n,t+n,w}(Y)/\BESS{f}_{n-1}^{s+n,t+n,w}(Y)$$
1005 where $\ZESS{f}_*^{*,*,*}$ and $\BESS{f}_*^{*,*,*}$ are  analogous to the notations in Notation \ref{nota:6fb333a2}, with the addition of an extra degree.
1006 
1007 In fact all the definitions and results in Section \ref{sec:e6be737c} remain valid in this synthetic setting, provided we include the extra weight degree in addition to the topological degree and ensure that all maps also preserve the weight degree. For convenience, we will not restate these results here but will occasionally refer to them and apply their synthetic versions when needed.
1008 
1009 \begin{notation} \label{not:deltaandrho}
1010 There are certain special maps between synthetic spectra that we want to consider. For any $n<m\le \infty$ and a spectrum $X$, we have the following  distinguished triangles of $\HF$-synthetic spectra
1011 $$\Sus^{0,-n}\nu X/\lambda^{m-n}\fto{\lambda^n} \nu X/\lambda^m\fto{~\rho_{n,m}~} \nu X/\lambda^n\fto{~\delta_{n,m}~} \Sus^{1,-n}\nu X/\lambda^{m-n}.$$
1012 We simply write $\rho=\rho_{n,m}$, $\delta=\delta_{n,m}$ by abuse of notation if $n,m$ is understood in the context.
1013 When $m=\infty$, this sequence is interpreted  as
1014 $$\Sus^{0,-n}\nu X\fto{\lambda^n} \nu X\fto{~\rho~} \nu X/\lambda^n\fto{~\delta~} \Sus^{1,-n}\nu X.$$
1015 
1016 Many arguments for finite $m$ implies the corresponding statement when $m=\infty$ because of
1017 $$\nu X=\varprojlim_{m} \nu X/\lambda^m$$
1018 as shown in \cite[Proposition A.13]{BHS}.
1019 
1020 We also note that our grading for the triangulation translation functor is smashing with $S^{1,0}$, which is consistent with \cite[Appendix A]{BHS} but is different from \cite[Section 7]{BurklundXu}.
1021 \end{notation}
1022 
1023 %First we show that there are no $\lambda^n$ or $\rho$-hidden extensions.
1024 
1025 \begin{proposition}\label{prop:9770ae6e} 
1026 The only nonzero differentials in the extension spectral sequences for the maps $\lambda^n$ and $\rho$ from Notation~\ref{not:deltaandrho} are the $d_0$'s:
1027 $$d^{\lambda^n}_0=\lambda^n \ \text{and} \ d^{\rho}_0=\rho.$$ 
1028 As a result, these $d_0$'s have no crossings.
1029 \end{proposition}
1030 \begin{proof}
1031     First, we show that the the following sequence is exact in the middle:
1032     \begin{equation}\label{eq:d8ee7ffe}
1033         \scalebox{1}{$E_\infty^{s,t,w}(\Sus^{0,-n}\nu X/\lambda^{m-n})\fto{\lambda^n} E_\infty^{s,t,w}(\nu X/\lambda^{m})\fto{\rho} E_\infty^{s,t,w}(\nu X/\lambda^{n})$}.
1034     \end{equation}
1035     We apply Proposition \ref{prop:30e8b746} and prove this case by case.
1036 
1037     When $t-w<0$ or $t-w\ge m$, the middle group $E_\infty^{s,t,w}(\nu X/\lambda^{m}) = 0$, so the sequence is exact in the middle.
1038     
1039     When $0\le t-w<n$, the sequence is isomorphic to
1040     $$0\to Z^{s,t}_{m-t+w}(X)/B^{s,t}_{1+t-w}(X)\to Z^{s,t}_{n-t+w}(X)/B^{s,t}_{1+t-w}(X)$$
1041     which is exact in the middle because the second map is clearly injective.
1042 
1043     When $n\le t-w<m$, the sequence is isomorphic to
1044     $$Z^{s,t}_{m-t+w}(X)/B^{s,t}_{1+t-w-n}(X)\to Z^{s,t}_{m-t+w}(X)/B^{s,t}_{1+t-w}(X)\to 0$$
1045     which is exact in the middle because the first map is clearly surjective.
1046 
1047     Thus, we have shown that (\ref{eq:d8ee7ffe}) is always exact in the middle. Combined with Corollary \ref{cor:aed3d1a4} we conclude that all $d^{\lambda^n}_i$, $d^{\rho}_i$ are trivial for $i>0$.
1048 \end{proof}
1049 
1050 \begin{remark}
1051     From the proof above, we see that in (\ref{eq:d8ee7ffe}), $\lambda^n$ is surjective or trivial, while $\rho$ is injective or trivial.
1052 \end{remark}
1053 
1054 However, the $\delta$-extension spectral sequence for
1055 $$\delta:\nu X/\lambda^n\to \Sus^{1,-n}\nu X/\lambda^{m-n}$$
1056 is more complicated, as it encodes classical Adams differentials $d_2$ through $d_m$.
1057 
1058 \begin{remark}
1059     For the convenience of readers to check gradings, whenever we write 
1060     $$d_{n}^{f}(x)=\lambda^k y$$
1061     for $f: \Sus^{m,w} \nu X\to \nu Y$,
1062     $x\in E_\infty^{s_1,t_1,w_1}(\nu X)$ and
1063     $y\in E_\infty^{s_2,t_2,w_2}(\nu Y)$,
1064     the following conditions must hold: 
1065     $$s_2=s_1+n, \ t_2-s_2=t_1-s_1+m, \ \text{and} \ w_2=w_1+w.$$
1066 \end{remark}
1067 
1068 \begin{proposition}\label{prop:6de7d130}
1069     Suppose in the classical Adams spectral sequence of $X$ we have $d_r(x)=y$, where $x\in Z_{r-1}^{s,t}(X)$ and $y\in Z_\infty^{s+r,t+r-1}(X)$. Consider the map
1070     $$\delta:\nu X/\lambda^n\to \Sus^{1,-n}\nu X/\lambda^{m-n}.$$
1071     \begin{enumerate}
1072         \item If $r\ge n+1$, then we view $x$ as an element of 
1073         $$E_\infty^{s,t,t}(\nu X/\lambda^n)\iso Z_n^{s,t}(X),$$
1074         and $\lambda^{r-n-1} y$ as an element of 
1075         $$E_\infty^{s+r,t+r-1, t+n}(\nu X/\lambda^{m-n})\iso Z_{m-r+1}^{s+r,t+r-1}(X)/B_{r-n}^{s+r,t+r-1}(X).$$
1076         We then have
1077         $$d^{\delta}_r(x)=\lambda^{r-n-1}y,$$
1078         which is trivial if $r>m$.
1079         \item If $r<n+1$, then we view $\lambda^{n+1-r}x$ as an element of 
1080         $$E_\infty^{s,t,t-n-1+r}(\nu X/\lambda^n)\iso Z^{s,t}_{r-1}(X)/B^{s,t}_{n+2-r}(X),$$
1081         and $y$ as an element of 
1082         $$E_\infty^{s+r,t+r-1, t+r-1}(\nu X)\iso Z_\infty^{s+r,t+r-1}(X).$$
1083         In this case, we have
1084         $$d^{\delta}_r(\lambda^{n+1-r}x)=y.$$
1085     \end{enumerate}
1086 \end{proposition}
1087 \begin{proof}
1088     Since $\delta=\delta_{n,m}$ is the composition of $\rho$ and $\delta_{n,\infty}$ as the following
1089      $$\xymatrix{
1090     \nu X/\lambda^n\ar[d]_-{\delta_{n,\infty}} \ar[rd]^{\delta_{n,m}} & \\
1091     \Sus^{1,-n}\nu X\ar[r]_-{\rho} & \Sus^{1,-n}\nu X/\lambda^{m-n}
1092     }$$
1093     it suffices to prove the case when $m=\infty$ by Corollary \ref{cor:290d35ce}. In the rest of the proof we will write $\delta_n=\delta_{n,\infty}$.
1094 
1095     First, we prove by induction on $n$ that
1096     \begin{equation}\label{eq:24e1e3f6}
1097         d^{\delta_n}_r(x)\equiv \lambda^{r-n-1}y\mod B_{r-1}^{s+r,t+r-1}(X)
1098     \end{equation}
1099     when $r\ge n+1$, and
1100     \begin{equation}\label{eq:2f8e024e}
1101         d^{\delta_n}_r(\lambda^{n+1-r}x)\equiv y\mod B_{r-1}^{s+r,t+r-1}(X)
1102     \end{equation}
1103     when $r\le n+1$. (These two expressions coincide when $r=n+1$.) For $n=1$, the claim holds since the $\tau$-Bockstein spectral sequence is isomorphic to the classical Adams spectral sequence. Now, assume $n\ge 2$ and the claim holds for $n-1$.
1104     
1105     Consider the following commutative diagram.
1106     $$\xymatrix{
1107         \Sus^{0,-1}\nu X/\lambda^{n-1} \ar[rd]_-{\delta_{n-1}} \ar[r]^-\lambda &  \nu X/\lambda^{n}\ar[d]^-{\delta_{n}}\\
1108         & \Sus^{1,-n}\nu X\\
1109     }$$
1110     By Corollary \ref{cor:166dc180}, if $r\le n$, we have
1111     $$d^{\delta_{n}}_r(\lambda^{n+1-r}x)=d^{\delta_{n-1}}_r(\lambda^{n-r}x)\equiv y\mod B_{r-1}^{s+r,t+r-1}(X).$$
1112     If $r\ge n+1$, $x$ can be viewed as an element of the $E_\infty$-pages of $\nu X/\lambda^{n-1}$ or $\nu X/\lambda^n$. We then have
1113     $$\lambda d^{\delta_{n}}_r(x)=d^{\delta_{n}}_r(\lambda x)=d^{\delta_{n-1}}_r(x)\equiv \lambda^{r-n}y \mod B_{r-1}^{s+r,t+r-1}(X)$$
1114     which implies
1115     $$d^{\delta_{n}}_r(x)\equiv\lambda^{r-n-1}y\mod B_{r-1}^{s+r,t+r-1}(X),$$
1116     since $r-n+1\le r-1$ and hence the indeterminacy $B_{r-n+1}$ introduced by dividing $\lambda$ is contained in $B_{r-1}$. The induction for (\ref{eq:24e1e3f6}) and (\ref{eq:2f8e024e}) is now complete.
1117 
1118     We will show that
1119     $B_{r-1}^{s+r,t+r-1}(X)$ in both equations are actually equal to the sum of images of $d^{\delta_{n}}_0$ through $d^{\delta_{n}}_{r-1}$.
1120     Consider any $y'\in B_{r-1}^{s+r,t+r-1}(X)$ and assume that $d_{r'}x'=y'$ is an essential classical Adams differential for $2\le r'\le r-1$. If $r\ge n+1$, using (\ref{eq:24e1e3f6}), we have
1121     $$d^{\delta_n}_{r'}(\lambda^{r-r'}x)\equiv \lambda^{r-n-1}y'\mod B_{r'-1}^{s+r,t+r-1}(X),$$
1122     and if $r\leq r'+1$, using (\ref{eq:2f8e024e}), we have
1123     $$d^{\delta_n}_{r'}(\lambda^{n+1-r'}x')\equiv y'\mod B_{r'-1}^{s+r,t+r-1}(X).$$
1124     Notice that the extra indeterminacy here is $B_{r'-1}$ instead of $B_{r-1}$. By induction on $r$, this shows that $B_{r-1}^{s+r,t+r-1}(X)$ equals the sum of images of $d^{\delta_{n}}_0$ through $d^{\delta_{n}}_{r-1}$.
1125     
1126     Therefore, we can omit $B_{r-1}^{s+r,t+r-1}(X)$ and simply write
1127     $$d^{\delta_n}_r(x)= \lambda^{r-n-1}y$$
1128     when $r\ge n+1$, and
1129     $$d^{\delta_n}_r(\lambda^{n+1-r}x)= y$$
1130     when $r\le n+1$.
1131 \end{proof}
1132 
1133 \begin{remark}
1134     The $d^{\delta}_r(x)$ we calculated in Proposition \ref{prop:6de7d130} may be inessential.
1135 \end{remark}
1136 
1137 
1138 \begin{corollary}\label{cor:2a636737}
1139     For $x,y,\delta$ in Proposition \ref{prop:6de7d130} we always have
1140     \begin{equation}\label{eq:841425e9}
1141         d^{\delta}_r(\lambda^ax)=\lambda^{a+r-n-1}y
1142     \end{equation}
1143     if $0\le a\le n$ and $0\le a+r-n-1<m-n$ (the differential is trivial if $a$ exceeds this range).
1144 \end{corollary}
1145 
1146 \begin{remark}\label{rmk:qvoewfj}
1147     As indicated in the proof, the right-hand side of equation (\ref{eq:841425e9}) (considered as a subset of $Z_\infty^{s+r,t+r-1}(X)$) is a coset of
1148     $$B_{r-1}^{s+r,t+r-1}(X)$$
1149     which is the same as the value of the classical Adams differential $d_r(x)=y$. This implies that the equation (\ref{eq:841425e9}) holds if and only if $d_r(x)=y$. Therefore the $\delta$-ESS encodes the same information as the classical Adams spectral sequence.
1150 \end{remark}
1151 
1152 \begin{example}
1153     We continue the discussion from Example~\ref{exam:synEinfty} regarding the implications of the classical Adams differentials in the 14-stem:
1154     $$d_2(h_4) = h_0h_3^2, \ d_3(h_0h_4) = h_0d_0.$$
1155     The reader is advised to begin by identifying the elements on the $E_\infty$-pages of the synthetic Adams spectral sequences for $S^{0,0}$ and $S^{0,0}/\lambda^k$ for $k=1,2,3$ in the relevant tridegrees, as illustrated in Example~\ref{exam:synEinfty}.
1156     
1157     For $\delta_1: S^{0,0}/\lambda \rightarrow S^{1,-1}$, we have
1158     $$d_2^{\delta_1}(h_4) = h_0h_3^2, \ d_3^{\delta_1}(h_0h_4) = \lambda h_0d_0.$$
1159     For $\delta_2: S^{0,0}/\lambda^2 \rightarrow S^{1,-2}$, we have
1160     $$d_2^{\delta_2}(\lambda h_4) = h_0h_3^2, \ d_3^{\delta_2}(h_0h_4) = h_0d_0, \ d_3^{\delta_2}(\lambda h_0h_4) = \lambda h_0d_0$$
1161     For $\delta_3: S^{0,0}/\lambda^3 \rightarrow S^{1,-3}$, we have
1162     $$d_2^{\delta_3}(\lambda^2 h_4) = h_0h_3^2, \ d_3^{\delta_3}(\lambda h_0h_4) = h_0d_0, \ d_3^{\delta_3}(\lambda^2 h_0h_4) =\lambda h_0d_0.$$
1163 \end{example}
1164 
1165 \begin{definition} \label{def:classicalcrossdiff}
1166     Suppose $r\ge n+1$ and $d_r(x)=y$, where
1167     $$x\in E_2^{s,t}(X), \hspace{0.5cm} y\in E_2^{s+r,t+r-1}(X).$$
1168     A crossing of $d_r(x)=y$ on the $E_{n+1}$-page refers to an essential Adams differential
1169     $$d_{r-a-b}(x^\prime)=y^\prime,$$
1170     where
1171     $$x^\prime\in E_2^{s+a,t+a}(X), \hspace{.5cm} y^\prime\in E_2^{s+r-b,t+r-b-1}(X),$$
1172     with $0<a\le n-1$ and $0\le b\le r-n-1$. See Figure \ref{fig:f5eae42a}.
1173 \end{definition}
1174 
1175 \begin{remark}
1176     The crossing defined here is opposite to crossings in Moss's theorem.
1177 \end{remark}
1178 \begin{figure}[h]
1179 \centering
1180 \scalebox{0.8}{\begin{tikzpicture}
1181     % Draw grid
1182     \fill[llgray] (1-0.5, 1-0.5) rectangle ++(1, 2);
1183     \fill[llgray] (0-0.5, 4-0.5) rectangle ++(1, 3);
1184     \tikzgrid{0}{2}{0}{7}
1185     \draw[thick] (1-0.5, 1-0.5) rectangle ++(1, 2);
1186     \draw[thick] (0-0.5, 4-0.5) rectangle ++(1, 3);
1187 
1188     % Draw bullets
1189     \coordinate (x) at (1, 0);
1190     \coordinate (y) at (0, 6);
1191     \coordinate (x1) at (1, 1); 
1192     \coordinate (y1) at (0, 5);
1193     
1194     \fill (x) circle (0.1) node[below right] {$x~$};
1195     \fill (x1) circle (0.1) node[below right] {$x'$};
1196     \fill (y) circle (0.1) node[left] {$y$};
1197     \fill (y1) circle (0.1) node[left] {$y'$};
1198 
1199     % Draw differentials
1200     \drawarrow (x) -- (y) node[near start, left] {$d_r$};
1201     \drawarrow (x1) -- (y1) node[near start, right] {$d_{r'}$}; 
1202 
1203     \node[anchor=east] at (-.7, 0) {$s$};
1204     \node[anchor=east] at (-.7, 1) {$s+a$};
1205     \node[anchor=east] at (-.7, 2) {$s+n-1$};
1206     \node[anchor=east] at (-.7, 3) {$s+n$};
1207     \node[anchor=east] at (-.7, 4) {$s+n+1$};
1208     \node[anchor=east] at (-.7, 5) {$s+r-b$};
1209     \node[anchor=east] at (-.7, 6) {$s+r$};
1210 \end{tikzpicture}}
